<meta>This entry describes the decorative binding of the manuscript, providing a visual introduction to the volume before the text begins.</meta>

# The Manuscript Binding

This volume is encased in a striking reddish-brown leather cover, showcasing the high artistry of early modern bookbinding. The decoration features elaborate <term>gold tooling</term> <note>A process where gold leaf is pressed into leather using heated metal stamps.</note> and <term>silver tooling</term>, creating a vibrant, reflective surface.

->**The Central Emblem**<-

At the heart of the cover lies a celestial motif: a gold-tooled sun with a human face, nestled within the curve of a silver-tooled crescent moon. This central design is enclosed within a double-lined octagonal frame. To add further detail, the points of the octagon are accented with small floral stamps and the <term>fleur-de-lis</term> <note>A stylized lily symbol, often associated with royalty or light.</note>.

Above and below this central octagon, large gold-tooled lilies command attention. The corners of the central rectangular field are finished with delicate, gold-tooled sprigs of leaves, softening the geometric structure of the design.

The entire composition is framed by a broad border featuring a repeating pattern of vines and leaves. This border was created using <term>blind tooling</term> <note>Impressions made in the leather without the use of gold or pigment, resulting in a subtle, recessed texture.</note>. A single, thin gold line traces the very outer edge of the cover, providing a clean finish to the decorative work.

The spine of the book, visible on the left, features raised bands—ridges formed by the cords used to sew the pages together—along with faint traces of gold decoration, indicating that this was intended to be a luxury object both on the shelf and in the hand.

<summary>A detailed description of the manuscript's ornate leather binding, featuring celestial sun and moon motifs and royal floral symbols in gold and silver.</summary>
<keywords>binding, leatherwork, gold tooling, celestial symbols, fleur-de-lis, book arts</keywords>

For what makes the Noble Chymical Art <note>original: "Chymische Kunst"; at this time, chemistry and alchemy were not yet distinct disciplines.</note> more hated than the great fraud of those vagabond, false charcoal-blowers <term>Kohlenbläser: a derogatory term for "puffers" or amateur alchemists who obsessed over the physical furnace and bellows without understanding the underlying philosophy</term>, who have perhaps looked a little into the fire, and on that basis boast of great things? # PROLOGUE.

## Concerning the Book of ISAAC HOLLANDUS, regarding the Philosophers' Stone <note>original: "Lapide Philosophorum"</note>.

In this prologue, you shall be taught which works everyone should guard themselves against.

My child, you should know that in this Art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy.</note> there are very many errors, especially in the Vegetable Work <term>Vegetable Work: Alchemical processes involving plants or plant-based substances, aimed at extracting their "essences" or "spirits."</term>, and even more so in the Animal Work <term>Animal Work: Alchemical processes involving materials derived from living creatures, such as blood, hair, or urine.</term>. The reason is this: in the vegetable work <note>original: "vegetabili opere"</note> there is much coagulating, dissolving, and rectifying <note>original: "rectificirens"; the process of purifying a liquid through repeated distillation.</note>; preparing the spirits <term>Spirit: In alchemy, a "spirit" is a volatile substance that easily evaporates or "flies away" when heated.</term> well so that they do not fly away, and also making many kinds of "strong water" <note>original: "starck Wasser"; usually refers to acids or powerful solvents like nitric acid.</note>, and fixing <term>Fixing: The process of making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to heat.</term> many watery spirits. To this belongs much governance <note>original: "Regiments"; refers to the "regimen" or the careful control and regulation of the heat of the furnace.</note> with various cares, to be so cautious that the spirits do not break the vessels; for through their fatty moisture <note>original: "feiste Feuchtigkeiten"; in alchemy, "fatty" or "oily" qualities were often associated with the sulfurous or flammable components of a substance.</note> they often cause the glasses to shatter if one gives them too much and too strong a fire when they are nearly fixed, and then they rise up,

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>This page begins the alchemical prologue of Isaac Hollandus, warning the student about the technical difficulties and physical dangers of the "Vegetable" and "Animal" works. It specifically emphasizes the risk of volatile "spirits" shattering glass equipment if the furnace heat is not carefully governed.</summary>
<keywords>Isaac Hollandus, Philosophers' Stone, Vegetable Work, Animal Work, laboratory safety, distillation, fixing spirits, alchemical furnace</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the prologue from page 1, where the author began warning the reader about the difficulties and "errors" inherent in the alchemical process. The previous page ended while discussing how spirits might behave when they are contained.</meta>

->Isaaci Hollandi<-

and stay enclosed within the glass; and by means of the rising <note>original: "Erhebens"; refers to the expansion of vapors or the process of sublimation where solids turn into gas and rise when heated.</note> they break the glass, so all is lost that one has put into it. One must guard against this, for it is a source of great worry—as those of you who have attempted such things well know. Great labor also belongs to the rectification <term>Rectification: The process of purifying a liquid, such as alcohol or "spirit of wine," through repeated distillation to reach its highest strength and purity.</term> of the wine and the long duration of time required. One must also perform great work to make flowing water <note>original: "$\nabla$ fl"; the symbol for water followed by 'fl' for 'fluens'. In this context, it refers to a specific distilled solvent or purified water.</note> and to preserve the spirits <term>Spirit: In alchemy, a "spirit" is a volatile substance that easily evaporates or "flies away" when heated.</term> well so that they do not fly away. This requires a long time, as is known to those who have attempted it further in digestion <term>Digestion: A process where a substance is subjected to gentle, continuous heat, similar to how food is broken down in a stomach, to mature the material.</term> and putrefaction <term>Putrefaction: A stage of the work where substances are allowed to decompose in moist heat, often turning black, which was considered necessary for spiritual "rebirth" of the matter.</term>. These processes must be handled very subtly, and a wondrous governance <term>Governance: Also called "regimen," this is the careful regulation and control of the heat of the alchemical furnace.</term> must be maintained—neither too weak nor too strong. One must always be on guard against this and preserve it with great care, as many are aware.

There is yet another concern: to calcine <term>Calcination: The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder or ash, known as "earth."</term> and improve the bodies <note>original: "corpora"; the solid materials or metals being used in the work.</note> and the earth, so that one does not burn them into glass or make them too dry. Therefore, one must maintain an especially good governance of the fire and be cautious in care and labor. Furthermore, one must calcine and clarify <note>The process of clearing a liquid or substance of impurities or sediment.</note> the earth, and distill the water over it again, and so forth with all the works <note>original: "operibus"; the various operations or stages of the alchemical process.</note> that belong to it.

Furthermore, in the composition <note>The stage where different purified elements are combined.</note>, one may easily give too much of one and too little of another. For if you have preserved your oiliness <note>original: "Faistigkeit"; refers to the "fatty" or oily essence of the substance, believed to be the combustible "soul" of the matter.</note> too well in the beginning, the medicine will have no ingress <term>Ingress: The ability of an alchemical substance to penetrate, enter, and transform another material, much like a dye soaking into a fabric.</term>; then you will be in worry with your

### Concerning the Philosopher’s Stone ###

A ij

Work and ruin; thus there are many dangers, great expenses, and even when we believe all things are well done and nothing is lacking among these prescribed things, we have perhaps not paid good enough attention, so that we have kept the fire <note>original: "Δ"; the alchemical symbol for fire.</note> too hot or too cold through negligence. If we then now wish to perform projection <term>Projection: The final stage of the alchemical process where the "Stone" or "Medicine" is cast upon a molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</term> with our work, and it does nothing as we had intended, and thus we find no use in it, we do not know what must be missing or how it may have happened; yet it comes from a lack of these prescribed manual operations which we have recounted here. Therefore I advise you, my children, that you do not venture to work in the **vegetable work** <term>Vegetable work: A branch of alchemy (vegetabili opere) that uses plants and organic matter as the starting material, rather than minerals or metals.</term> for the reason recounted before; for because of one small thing the work may be ruined, and then all cost is lost. Therefore I advise you to stay completely clear of the vegetable-work because of the separation of the elements—**separation** and **rectification**—which are necessary therein, and the much evil worry for a long time, as well as the many kinds of uncertainty which may occur therein. The time required extends so long that one almost knows no end to the work.

If in the beginning you lose the natural moisture, then your work will become too dry in the composition; or if you lose some **spirit of air** <note>original: "Spiritum aeris"; refers to the volatile gaseous substance or "breath" released during the process.</note> during distillation due to a defect in the vessel or the **luting** <term>Luting: A clay or cement (Latin: luti) used to seal the joints of alchemical apparatus to prevent vapors from escaping.</term>, then you lose your high **projection**. If you lose some of the fire, then your **Medicine** <note>The "Medicine" is another name for the Philosopher's Stone in its curative or transformative state.</note> will not be deeply colored, and you lose—

<meta>catchword: also</meta>

<summary>The author warns against the "vegetable work" due to its complexity and the high risk of failure from minor errors in heat control or equipment sealing.</summary>
<keywords>fire, vegetable work, projection, rectification, spirit of air, luting, distillation, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s warning about the delicate balance of elements required for the alchemical "Stone." It follows the previous page’s discussion on the "spirit of air" and the moisture necessary for a successful composition.</meta>

->### Isaac Hollandus###<-

also [affects] its penetration; if it has too much air <note>original: "aeris"</note>, it flies away. If it then has too much water <note>original: "$\nabla$"; the alchemical symbol for water.</note>, it drowns the spirits so that they cannot be fixed <term>Fixed: To "fix" a substance (fixation) means to transform it from a volatile or evaporating state into a solid, stable form that can endure the heat of a fire without vanishing.</term>. If it then has too much earth, it is too dry, so that it has no melting capacity and no penetration.

Therefore, it is a very delicate matter to work in the **separation of the elements**, for many failures occur there, as well as in **rectification** <term>Rectification: The process of purifying a substance, usually a liquid, through repeated distillation to remove impurities and concentrate its power.</term>. If even the smallest part of the elements is spoiled, no matter how little it may be, the work is lost. Therefore I advise you, dear sons <note>original: "Filii"; a traditional term for students of alchemy, often called "Sons of Art."</note>, who shy away from all operations where one must separate the elements—whether it be in the **animal, mineral, or vegetable work**, or in the **Stone** which God has given us for free—take heed when the preparation of the Stone depends upon separation. Avoid your work, however, for the sake of its uncertainty; for it happens very easily in the beginning or the middle that one may lose some of its moisture, and then all labor is lost. For when one tries to put the components back together, they cannot be unified; likewise, its penetration <term>Ingress: Also called "entry," this is the alchemical property that allows the fermented "Stone" to permeate and transform every part of a base metal into gold or silver.</term> is taken away, and then both expense and labor are lost.

There have been seven <note>original: "$\lambda$"; this shorthand symbol likely refers to the "Seven Masters" of the art, corresponding to the seven planetary metals.</note> masters who performed this work, but they did it only to fathom the depths of nature; for they had already completed and prepared the **Great Work** beforehand, so they did not worry about the costs or the labor. This required much investigation, and we shall—God willing—discuss this Great Work further.

There are also other artists and philoso-

<meta>catchword: phi [philosophers]</meta>

<summary>Isaac Hollandus warns that an imbalance of elements—too much air, water, or earth—will prevent the Philosopher's Stone from properly "fixing" or "penetrating" metals. He cautions his "sons" against the extreme difficulty and risk of failure inherent in the separation and rectification of elements.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s warning about the delicate balance of moisture and heat. It follows the previous page’s discussion on how incorrect proportions of air, water, or earth can cause the alchemical work to fail by preventing "ingress" or penetration.</meta>

# Concerning the Philosophers’ Stone <note>original: "De lapide Philosophico"</note> #

[Philosophers] have been, who have worked on wonderful **amalgamations**, **fixations**, **multiplications**, and many other wonderful **operations**, taking on what they believe to be "short works"—of perhaps one month, or eight to ten weeks—which nevertheless stretch out so long that they never reach the end due to failures that occur in the work. For in the beginning or the middle of the work, they do not preserve its natural moisture, so that their work becomes completely dry; or they cook it too hard, or too cold, or too long over the fire <term>△: The alchemical symbol for fire.</term> so that it loses its **ingress** <term>Ingress: The ability of an alchemical substance to penetrate or "enter" into a metal to transform its properties.</term> and melting capacity. Thus, they expect to gain a profit from it, but nothing is found, and they remain as far from the end as they were before; cost, time, and everything is lost.

Therefore, shun all **amalgamations** or **multiplications** and **fixations**, even if you believe them to be quite short. They are actually so long and must be done with such great care and labor that one cannot reach the end, alongside the uncertainty regarding the washing and purification that must take place. For no **fixation** <term>Fixation: The process of making a volatile (evaporating) substance stable so it can endure heat without vanishing.</term> can occur before the **spirit** and **body** <note>original: "Spiritus und corpus"</note> have first been purified of all dregs <note>original: "fecibus"; the sediment or waste matter left after a process.</note>. But no fixation or new "entry" <note>An alternative term for "ingress."</note> can occur if, during the washing, purification, and dissolving <note>original: "solvirung"</note>, you deprive the spirit of its moisture; if so, all is lost.

Thus I advise that you avoid all **operations** in which washing and cleaning...

<sig>A iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...occur [schehen]</meta>

<summary>The author warns against "short" alchemical processes like amalgamation and fixation, noting that they often fail due to the loss of natural moisture or improper heating. He emphasizes that the 'spirit' and 'body' must be purified of dregs before any stable transformation can occur.</summary>
<keywords>amalgamation, fixation, multiplication, ingress, moisture, fire, purification, spirit and body, dregs</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on the failures inherent in "short works" of alchemy. The preceding text warned that practitioners often lose their "ingress" and moisture by over-firing their materials, leading to the loss of time and money.</meta>

-> # Of Isaac Hollandus # <-

[must] occur, because of the care, burden, time, and costs that belong to it; or also when the separation of the elements must occur, or distillation, or calcination, or coagulation—because of the danger that may arise therein, which I have partly recounted thus far and which is well known to the practitioners <note>original: "laboranten"; refers to the hands-on workers in an alchemical laboratory.</note>. Therefore, do not be hasty to begin any one of these recounted works. For if only a single one of the prescribed failures should occur—of which there are a thousand times more than we have set down or can recount because of the great length of time required—then all cost, labor, and long time would be lost, and you would fall into impatience.

Therefore, hold yourselves to the Great Art, or to the Great Elixir, as your ancestors have done. And once you have accomplished and completed this, you may then attempt other operations of nature. If you do otherwise, you are not following my advice. In the beginning, take up the Great Work for yourselves, for there is no worry in it. One distills, dissolves, coagulates, or purifies nothing within it <note>The author suggests that in the "Great Work," these processes happen naturally or simultaneously within a single vessel, rather than as separate, dangerous steps.</note>; there are no foreign works, nor foreign things, nor impure things. You have also [not] calcined the dregs <term>feces: the solid remains or "dregs" left over after a substance has been processed.</term> for it is not necessary. You separate no element, for they are pure. It is one lineage, one thing, one vessel, one furnace, and one work for the **white** and for the **red** <note>These refer to the two main stages of the Philosophers' Stone: the "White" stage (Albedo) which transmutes metals into silver, and the "Red" stage (Rubedo) which produces gold.</note>.

Therefore, no danger can befall this work; it is nothing other than **woman’s work** <term>Woman’s work: A common alchemical phrase (Latin: *opus mulierum*) suggesting that once the initial stages are set, the process requires only gentle heat and patience, much like the domestic tasks of cooking or laundry.</term> and

<summary>Isaac Hollandus advises students to avoid the complex and error-prone "minor" alchemical operations and instead focus on the "Great Work," which he describes as a singular, safer, and more natural process. He characterizes the final stages of the Great Work as "woman's work," implying it requires patience and gentle heat rather than strenuous labor.</summary>

# Concerning the Philosopher's Stone #

You should know that in all things that God has created, the true Art is within. Therefore, the Ancients say the Art is in all things, and it is true. You should understand it thus: every specific thing has its <term>perfect medicine <note>original: "perfectam Medicinam"; in alchemy, this refers to the purified essence or "soul" of a substance that can heal or perfect its own kind.</note></term> within itself, but it is unprepared. If you are able to prepare it, then you do not need to buy or possess any foreign <term>medicines <note>original: "medicamina."</note></term>. This is to be understood for all: no sickness on earth can befall a person for which they do not have the perfect medicine within themselves, through which they may recover entirely, if only they know how to prepare it well. They may take it from themselves and prepare it without any lessening of their strength or damage to their body.

This is also to be understood regarding all animals and birds, and all plants—everything that God has created is so. But the ignorant cannot understand what the Ancients have said; they intend to make a medicine for metals out of all things. Therefore, they take eggs, blood, urine, and the like, and intend thereby to bring <term>imperfect bodies <note>original: "imperfecta corpora"; base metals such as lead or copper, which alchemists believed were "unfinished" versions of gold.</note></term> into <term>perfection <note>the state of being gold or silver.</note></term>. And when they have worked in this way, they are as close as they were at the start, and they persist in their folly.

But my child should know that a human <term>begets <note>original: "generirt."</note></term> a human, a horse a horse, a bird a bird—each after its own kind. Otherwise, it would be against nature and all reason that one should be able to make metal out of any such <term>seed <note>original: "sperma"; here referring to biological reproductive fluids.</note></term>, blood, or eggs, where there...

<meta>catchword: not</meta>

<summary>Isaac Hollandus explains that every created thing contains its own "perfect medicine" within it, emphasizing that alchemical transformation must follow the laws of nature—where like begets like—rather than using unrelated biological materials like blood or eggs to create gold.</summary>

### Isaac Hollandus ###

No foreign things that are not of its nature should enter into the **Stones**, though some wish to bring such things into its nature. However, it is impossible for them to work in such a way that they <term>fix <note>original: "figiren"; in alchemy, "fixation" is the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it does not evaporate.</note></term> the matter. Even when they have fixed it, they are just as close to the goal as they were before; for then it is nothing other than earth that has lost its moisture, is <term>infusible <note>original: "unflüssig"; a substance that will not melt or flow, making it useless for "penetrating" and transforming other metals.</note></term>, and has no <term>entrance <note>original: "Eingang"; the ability of the alchemical medicine to penetrate and transform a base metal.</note></term>. They stop exactly where they should have first begun to work. 

But if they knew of what lineage the Stone was, and recognized its <term>Father and Mother, Sister and Brother <note>The alchemist uses family metaphors to describe the chemical relationship between substances—usually referring to the union of Sulphur (Father) and Mercury (Mother).</note></term>, arm in arm, mouth in mouth, they would die <note>Refers to "mortification" or "putrefaction," a stage in the alchemical process where the substance "dies" or turns black before being reborn in a purified state.</note> at that very hour. If they recognized and understood these things, they would achieve all their desires through the **Art**, and all their works would turn out successfully.

Dear <term>Sons <note>original: "Filij" (Latin: filii); a traditional way for alchemical masters to address their students.</note></term>, I have revealed all of this to you in bright, clear words. Therefore, do not begin any <term>foreign operations <note>original: "frembde operationes"; chemical procedures using substances that are not part of the "nature" of the Stone.</note></term> before you have completed the **Great Work**. Afterward, begin whatever you wish, and neither cost nor time will hinder you, for you will have everything according to your pleasure. I have told you of many failures that may occur, of which there are many more; but you should know that there is a simple rule about which the **Philosophers** all speak so strangely, giving <term>parables <note>original: "Gleichnüs" and "parabolas."</note></term> and setting them forth with hidden names. Yet they all mean the **Great Work**, and those who can perform the Great Work can also understand all the parables and hidden words.

### Isaac Hollandus ###

...is nothing / therefore seek to extract nothing from imperfect things <note>The author refers back to the "eggs, blood, and urine" mentioned on the previous page as substances that cannot produce true gold.</note> to transmute metals into gold <note>original: "☉" (Sol)</note> and silver <note>original: "☽" (Luna)</note> / for that is against reason and nature / and is impossible. Furthermore / gold and silver also become a medicine without separation of the elements / labor / danger / or accident / also one can make a perfect medicine / out of the **two stones** / which God has given us for free / without special...

### On the Philosophers' Stone ###
<note>original: "De lapide Philosophico"</note>

...much preparation or separation / and my child <note>A common term of endearment for an alchemical apprentice.</note> shall understand / that all these prescribed works concern the **Great Work** <term>Great Work<note>The "Magnum Opus," the traditional name for the process of creating the Philosophers' Stone.</note></term> / without much boiling and preparation / and without special costs and foreign things / although they are a long time in the preparation / yet they are certain.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process using the allegory of a family being purified, united, and "dying" in a sealed vessel. It outlines the cycle of distillation—rising as vapor and falling as "dew"—to produce the final "Emperor," the red Philosophers' Stone.</summary>

# Here begin the Works of the Ancients with all perfect instruction / nothing
## left out.

Now, dear **sons** <note>original: "Filij" (Latin: filii). Alchemical authors traditionally addressed their readers as "sons" to signify a spiritual or initiatory lineage.</note>, we wish to write of the **Great Work** <term>Great Work<note>The "Magnum Opus," the laboratory process of creating the Philosophers' Stone.</note></term> / which our ancestors have used / in many manners and forms / and they have all come to one end <note>The "end" refers to the shared goal of transmutation.</note> / but their **stone** did not perform the same **projection** <term>projection<note>The final act of alchemy, where a small amount of the Stone is "projected" or thrown into molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note></term> / but rather one higher than the other / according to how they had made the work subtle / accordingly it was also of high color / the ancients worked a long time before they brought forth the stone / and have shortened the work with subtlety / just as work is still done every day / and understand / our ancestors required three or four years / before they perfected their stone / for at that time one knew of no **Strong Water** <term>Strong Water<note>original: "starcken Wasser"; refers to powerful mineral acids like nitric acid, which could dissolve metals far faster than organic acids.</note></term> / but only of distilled vinegar / now the descendants have invented **Aqua Fortis** <term>Aqua Fortis<note>original: "AF"; Latin for "Strong Water," specifically nitric acid. Its discovery was a technological revolution in alchemy.</note></term> / that has greatly shortened the work / and you should know / that one may yet shorten the work / with the first labor / by making the **metals** <note>original: "Metalla"</note> subtle and uniting the **spirits** <term>spirits<note>original: "Spiritus"; the volatile, active components of a substance, such as mercury or sulfur, which were thought to animate the "dead" body of the metal.</note></term> well / so that the **matter** <note>original: "materia"</note> alone may proceed / and that one makes and mixes the wet with the dry so subtly / that it becomes a **dough** <note>Alchemists often aimed for a "pasty" or "dough-like" consistency during the marriage of the "Fixed" (dry) and "Volatile" (wet) principles.</note>.

Therefore <note>This is a catchword, indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>Isaac Hollandus explains how the discovery of Aqua Fortis (nitric acid) revolutionized alchemy by shortening the process of making the Philosophers' Stone from years to a much shorter duration. He emphasizes that the goal remains the same as that of the ancients, but modern "subtlety" and better solvents allow for faster results.</summary>

# Of the Philosophers' Stone.
<note>original: "De lapide Philosophico"</note>

Therefore, says **Hermes the Philosopher** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure regarded as the father of alchemy.</note>, be not sluggish in the beginning of your work / and clean all your things well and clearly / join them subtly / so that you may rejoice afterwards / **Geber**, **Dandin**, and **Morienus** <note>Famous historical alchemists: Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), Morienus (a legendary 7th-century hermit), and Dandin (likely a corruption of a medieval name).</note> also say: unite the water well with the earth / and the moist with the dry / so that you may afterwards see the blackness of the sea / that is / its black color / which you shall see in the <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The stage of rotting or "death" of the matter, considered the essential first step toward its "resurrection" into the Stone.</note>, which shall occur in 24 days with a small fire / which is a good sign of a good union.

Therefore, my child, be diligent in the beginning / so that you prepare your <term>matter</term> <note>original: "materiam"; the raw physical substance being transformed.</note> diligently and well / for once it is set in the fire / you have already done all your work / and need not worry about anything else / but only to govern the fire / you should know that I will hereafter describe / various operations which are altogether perfect. First, our ancestors worked on amalgamations of **gold** <note>original: ☉, the symbol for the Sun or Gold.</note> and **silver** <note>original: ☾, the symbol for the Moon or Silver.</note> / which is a very perfect work / but yet very long / though otherwise the safest and with fewer worries / they also applied <term>amalgamation</term> <note>The process of blending a metal with mercury to create a soft paste.</note> in many manners / but all came to a certain end / yet not to the same height of <term>projection</term> <note>The final act of transmutation where the Stone is cast upon base metal to turn it into gold.</note> / and one shortened his work with subtlety more than the other / as we shall teach in the <term>vegetable work</term> <note>original: "vegetabili Werck"; alchemical processes involving plant-like growth or organic substances.</note>.

# The First Work of the Ancients.

Know, dear **sons** <note>original: "Filij"; a traditional alchemical way of addressing students or initiates.</note>, that there have been some ancients / who took fine **silver** <note>original: ☾, the symbol for Luna or silver.</note> from **lead** <note>original: ♄, the symbol for Saturn or lead.</note> very well refined in the crucible / filed to the highest subtlety, 3 <term>Loth</term> <note>A Germanic unit of weight; one Loth is approximately 14–17 grams or half an ounce.</note> / and fine **gold** <note>original: ☉, the symbol for Sol or gold.</note> / purified through the <term>royal cement</term> <note>original: "cement regale"; a dry refining process used to purify gold, typically using salt or brick dust to remove impurities.</note>, also finely filed / 1 Loth / and 8 Loth of well-purified **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurij".</note>.
This they amalgamated well in an iron mortar with a steel pestle / and thus rubbed it for 12 or 14 hours / afterward they put it into a stone or glass vessel formed thus [image-desc: small woodcut of a square furnace or oven] / and set it in sand / so hot that one could not easily endure a finger in the sand / and let it stand thus / so that the moisture might evaporate / in the morning they found the **matter** <note>original: "materiam".</note> hard / they put it back into the mortar / and gave it another half-part of mercury, or 4 Loth / so that the **inner substance** <note>original: "intima".</note> became uniform / this manual labor they performed for so long / until the matter could pass through a double linen cloth while dry / afterward they set it for 8 more days with the vessel in sand and rubbed the matter every day in the mortar for 8 hours without ceasing / when the 8 days had passed / then take the matter and put it into such a glass / press upon the mouth a ground [image-desc: woodcut of an alchemical flask or retort with a long neck, standing on a base or tripod] little glass / which seals well / and put a weight upon it / so that it remains lying / set it on a **tripod** <note>original: "tripodem"; a three-legged stand used to hold a vessel over a fire.</note>, and

It emphasizes the physical preparation of the "matter" through grinding and evaporation before the substance is sealed for further heating.</summary>

# Concerning the Philosopher's Stone. <note>original: "De lapide Philosophico."</note>

# Concerning the Philosophers’ Stone. <note>original: "De lapide Philosophico"</note>

In this art there are many errors / for many are those /
who undertake to make the **Stone of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophorum"</note> /
and with good **governance of the fire** <note>original: "Regiment des Fewers"; the careful management of heat over long periods.</note> they bring it to this degree /

and when the **matter** <note>original: "materia"</note> or Stone is fixed / they then expect to perform **projection** <note>The final act of alchemical transformation where a small amount of the "Stone" is cast upon a larger mass of base metal to transmute it into gold or silver.</note> /
and they throw it upon raw **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurij"</note> or other **imperfect metals** <note>original: "imperfecta metalla"; metals such as lead or tin which alchemists believed were "sick" or unfinished versions of gold.</note>, but find no use / then they fall into despair / and say the Art is impossible / and it is true / for them it is impossible / for they have Earth / which has lost its moisture / just as **Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a famous polymath and alchemist whose Latinized works were foundational to European alchemy.</note> says / spirits that have lost their moisture / through many **sublimations** <note>original: "sublimationibus"; the process of vaporizing a solid and collecting the deposit.</note> and **fixations** <note>original: "fixationibus"</note>, they do no good / so long as they are Earths / and so dry / as this one is / and the ignorant do not understand it / for once they have made their stone / and united it well / so that it is in its [proper] colors / as it is meant to be / [they do not know] that it must thereafter be made **subtle** <note>Refined into a more penetrating, ethereal form.</note> again / and **volatile** [fleeting] / if there is to be an **ingress** <note>original: "ingreß"; the ability of the alchemical medicine to "enter" and permeate the body of the base metal.</note> or **projection** / but they do not understand the words of the Wise / and well they know that one makes the Stone / as was previously told / and they make it just as it must be made / but they let the work hang / just when they should truly begin / and thus they remain in their mad error.

### Isaac Hollandus

This Art / which has been revealed to me / therefore I charge you by the living Son of God / that you do not reveal this secret / except to your own sons / provided it seems to you / that they possess the love of God / so that your soul and mine are not damned because of it / on account of the great burden that might come from it / open your eyes and ears / see and hear the great mystery / that is in Nature / that all **Philosophical Stones** <note>original: "lapides Philosophicos"; refers to the various stages or types of the legendary substance capable of transmutation.</note> / in whatever manner they are composed / in the Great Work / of which we speak here / both for the **White and for the Red** <note>The "White" stone (albedo) transmutes base metals into silver, while the "Red" stone (rubedo) transmutes them into gold.</note> / can be completed and perfected in one vessel and one furnace.

Now someone might ask / why then does one take <term>Gold</term> and <term>Silver</term> together into the Work; that is done for the reason / that <term>Gold</term> is **fixed** <note>Stable and resistant to the fire.</note> / and thus the Work becomes all the shorter / if your <term>Silver</term> were fixed in the Work / then the Stone for the White would be made ; But now the <term>Silver</term> is not fixed / and before it becomes fixed / much time is required for it / and much **cooking** <note>original: "Kochung"; the process of sustained heating or digestion.</note> / for the <term>Silver</term> must be fixed / before it **fixes** <note>original: "figirt"; to make a volatile substance stable.</note> the **Mercury** / and for that reason one puts <term>Gold</term> and <term>Silver</term> together in the Work / you may also make both stones from **Mercury** and <term>Gold</term> alone / and that would happen even sooner /

# Isaac Hollandus

Know now / that if the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosophers' Stone, the miraculous substance sought by alchemists.</note> were liquid / then one could not bring out its <term>Redness</term> <note>The "Red" stage (rubedo) is the final perfection of the alchemical work, representing the highest potency.</note> / for when it is glowing hot / it would melt / and penetrate right through the glass / and thus be lost / for at the end it must glow for at least 3 days. Upon this it is now to be noted / that the Stone must first be made in such a way / before one makes it fusible <note>Capable of being melted.</note> / the ignorant cannot understand / nor notice this / for they do not recognize Nature / and thus both the White and the Red Stone must be made / before one makes them liquid and subtle / as you may then well grasp such things in your own understanding.

->Now hear, my sons, the great <term>Secret</term> <note>original: "Secretum"; Latin for "secret."</note>.<-

Which is in the <term>Art</term> <note>"The Art" refers to the practice of alchemy itself.</note> / and which has never been put into any writing / except by me alone / how one should prepare the two Stones and make them fusible. In order to perform high <term>projection</term> <note>The final act of the Great Work, where a tiny piece of the Stone is "projected" or thrown into molten base metal to transmute it into gold or silver.</note>.

Take your Stone / and pour over it clear, clean <term>Paradise-Water</term> <note>original: "Paradiess-Wasser"; a symbolic name for a highly purified solvent or "mercurial water" used to dissolve the Stone.</note> / and unite it with the water / put it in a <term>prison</term> <note>original: "Kerker"; a technical term for a sealed glass vessel from which no spirit can escape.</note> / and seal it tight / then it will rise toward Heaven in the <term>Circle of the Moon</term> <note>original: "Circul des Monden"; likely referring to the upper, cooler part of the alchemical vessel where vapors condense.</note> / and will turn into <term>Dew</term> <note>original: "Taw"; condensed liquid resulting from distillation.</note> / and fall down again drop by drop / according to the teaching of the Masters / and will moisten the earth so that it shall bring forth

# On the Philosophers' Stone
<note>original: "De lapide Philosophico"</note>

### Of the Philosophical Stone <note>original: De lapide Philosophico</note>

...sublimate <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...then you incerated it with well-purified Mercury."</note>, thereafter take it out and keep it in a glass or box, in a warm place, well closed, so that moisture does not draw into it again.

And my child shall know that one must prepare all <term>Mercury <note>original symbol: ☿</note></term> thus, with which one wishes to <term>amalgamate <gloss>to blend mercury with another metal</gloss></term>, otherwise it will not "die" or be <term>fixed <gloss>rendered solid and resistant to heat</gloss> <note>original: figiret</note></term>. This has misled many, so that they have not been able to reach their goal because of the superfluity of fire which it <note>the mercury</note> possesses, and thus they remain in error.

Furthermore, know that <term>Gold <note>original symbol: ☉</note></term> and <term>Silver <note>original symbol: ☾</note></term> and Mercury must be prepared in this way for the sake of a better union. Now you shall weigh your powder of Gold and Silver, and take as much <term>prepared Mercury <note>original: ☿ præparati</note></term> as it weighs, and no more. For if you take more, your work must stand longer in the fire; but if there is no more of one than the other, then the spirit "dies" <note>loses its volatile, evaporating nature</note> easily with the body and becomes fixed, and is brought to its perfect color, whether it be to the White <note>the stage for making silver</note> or to the Red <note>the stage for making gold</note>. And thereafter, pour over it a great quantity of Mercury to <term>incerate <gloss>to soften a substance to a wax-like consistency</gloss> <note>original: inceriren</note></term> it, and to make the <term>Stone <note>original: lapidem</note></term> fluid.

Upon the fixed white Stone, you must pour for one part well 10 or 12 parts of Spirit; and upon the Red well 20 or 24, up to 30 parts. For if you take 12 parts of Spirit for 1 part of the White, the Red requires well 30 parts before it becomes meltable. For the reason is: the white Stone is raw,

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the "inceration" or softening of the alchemical Stone. ### Isaac Hollandus

...moist, cold, and white; therefore, it does not require half as much Paradise-Water <note>An alchemical term for a highly purified solvent or distilled "spirit" used to dissolve the Stone.</note> or Spirit to <term>incerate</term> <gloss>to soften a substance to a waxy consistency</gloss> it as the Red [Stone] does. The Red Stone is hot and dry in its nature, and before it can become meltable, it requires at least 30 parts of Spirit for every 1 part of Stone.

Now, one might ask: if one must pour so much Paradise-Water onto the white and the red powder before they will become liquid and subtle, then surely it must stand in the fire for a very long time before all the Spirit can become "ripe" and "die" <note>In alchemy, "dying" refers to a volatile substance losing its ability to evaporate and becoming "fixed" or solid.</note> with the Stone to become fixed. And this is why: in the innermost composition, both the Spirit and the <term>Body</term> <note>original: corpus</note> were raw; therefore, the mixture must stand for a longer time before they can <term>perfect</term> <note>original: perficiren</note> one another and embrace each other in their foundations.

Now, one must maintain the first composition with such a small fire that little or nothing at all may rise up <note>evaporate or sublimate</note> within the vessel in which it stands. It must remain thus for a long time before it can "die" and become a powder. Once the Stone for the White and the Red is made, it is fixed, but it is not yet liquid. Although it is a <term>Medicine</term> <gloss>a powerful elixir or transformative substance</gloss> in its foundation, it does not yet have an <term>entrance</term> <note>original: ingreß; the ability of the Stone to penetrate and transform other metals.</note>.

Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> says that when <term>Mercury</term> <note>original symbol: ☿</note> is <term>dissolved</term> <note>original: solvirt</note>, it easily dissolves other metals and spirits. And when it is <term>coagulated</term> <note>original: coagulirt; thickened or turned from a liquid/vapor into a solid</note>, it quickly continues to coagulate. Hermes further says that even if a great deal of Paradise-Water is poured over it—even as much as 30 parts to 1 part of <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: Merc:</note> that is already coagulated—the Stone will easily continue to coagulate that additional Paradise-Water.

<summary>This page describes the delicate proportions and heating required to "incerate" the alchemical Stone, explaining why the Red Stone requires more spirit than the White. It emphasizes the need for a slow fire to ensure the "Spirit" and "Body" bond properly into a fixed "Medicine."</summary>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the "inceration" and fixation of the alchemical Stone. Following the previous page’s discussion on the proportions of "Paradise-Water" (a solvent) needed for the White and Red stages, this section explains why the heat must be managed differently in the later stages of the process compared to the beginning.</meta>

->### On the Philosopher’s Stone<-

Another reason is that one part is already a **Medicine**, yet it is unmeltable; therefore, it draws the **Paradise-Water** <note>A highly purified alchemical solvent or "spirit."</note> slightly toward its own nature. Furthermore, when one pours the **Paradise-Water** onto the **powder** and places it in the furnace, one may give it much more heat than at the beginning of the work. In the beginning, it must not <term>sublimate</term> <gloss>to transition from a solid to a vapor and back to a solid without becoming liquid</gloss>; but at this stage, one may give it enough heat so that it rises up and falls back down in drops onto the powder. As soon as it begins to **die** <note>In alchemy, "dying" refers to the substance losing its volatility and becoming "fixed" or solid.</note> and becomes a powder, then increase your fire so that it sublimates, and turn the glass over often so that the top part comes to the bottom. Do this until it all remains together at the bottom. Afterward, increase your fire slightly until it reaches its color, whether it be the **White** or the **Red**.

These are the reasons why one pours so much **Paradise-Water** onto one part of the **powder**. It does not need to stand for as long as it did at the beginning of the work; therefore, one takes no more of one than the other. I tell you these reasons so that you may gain a correct understanding of the work, so that you may know the good from the bad and understand what you are doing, so that you do not fall into error. Therefore, be cautious and recognize this; lock it in your heart and **memory** <note>original: memoriam</note>, and reflect upon what might be harmful to you in the work before you begin anything.

Now we shall set to the work. Take the powder of **Gold** <note>represented by the symbol ☉</note> and **Silver** <note>represented by the symbol ☾</note>; weigh it; take as much **Philosophers' Mercury** <note>original: Merc: Philosophorum</note>, as mentioned above <note>original: ut supra</note>, and put it into

### Isaac Hollandus

...moist, cold, and white; therefore, it does not require half as much Paradise-Water or Spirit to <term>incerate</term> <gloss>to treat a substance until it reaches a waxy, meltable consistency</gloss> it as the Red [Stone] does. The Red Stone is hot and dry in its nature, and before it can become meltable, it likely requires 30 parts of Spirit for every 1 part of the Stone. Now one might ask: if one must pour so much Paradise-Water upon the white and red powders before they will become fluid and subtle, then they must stand in the fire for a very long time before the Spirit can be fully ripened and "die" with the Stone to become fixed <note>In alchemy, to "fix" a substance is to make it resistant to fire so that it no longer evaporates.</note>. And this is why: in the innermost composition, both the Spirit and the <term>body</term> <note>original: *corpus*</note> were raw, and therefore it must stand longer before they can <term>perfect</term> <note>original: *perficiren*</note> one another and circulate within their foundations.

Now, one must maintain the first composition with such a small fire that little or nothing may rise within the vessel in which it stands. Thus it must stand for a long time before it can die and become a powder. Once the Stone has been made into the White and the Red, it is fixed, but it is not yet fluid; even though it is a Medicine in its foundation, it does not yet have an <term>entrance</term> <note>original: *ingress*; the ability for the alchemical medicine to penetrate and transform other metals.</note>.

Hermes <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistos, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> says: when the **Mercury** <note>represented by the symbol ☿</note> is <term>dissolved</term> <note>original: *solvirt*</note>, it easily dissolves other **metals** <note>original: *Metalla*</note> and Spirit; and when it is <term>congealed</term> <note>original: *coagulirt*</note>, it soon congeals further. Hermes says further: even if a great deal of Paradise-Water is poured over it—even 30 parts to 1 part of **Mercury** <note>original: *Merc:*</note> that is already congealed—it will continue to congeal that other Paradise-Water easily.

### On the Philosophical Stone

Another reason is that one part is already a Medicine, though it is infusible <note>meaning it cannot yet be melted like a metal</note>; therefore, it easily draws the Paradise-Water into its own nature. Also, when the Paradise-Water is poured onto the powder and placed in the furnace, one may apply much more heat than at the beginning of the Work. In the beginning, the substance must not <term>sublime</term> <gloss>to heat a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, often used for purification</gloss>. But at this stage, one may give it enough heat so that it rises and falls back down in drops upon the powder. As soon as it begins to "die" <note>In alchemy, the "death" of a substance refers to its transformation or loss of its previous form to reach a higher state.</note> and becomes a powder, increase your fire so that it sublimes, and turn the glass vessel often so that the top part comes to the bottom. Do this until it all remains together at the bottom. After that, increase your fire slightly until it reaches its color, whether it be the White or the Red.

These are now the reasons why one pours so much Paradise-Water onto one part of the powder. It does not need to sit for as long as it did at the beginning of the Work; therefore, one takes no more of one than of the other. I tell you this reason so that you may grasp a true understanding of the Work, so that you know the good from the bad and understand what you are doing, lest you fall into error. Therefore, be cautious and recognize these things; lock them in your heart and <term>memory</term> <note>original: *memoriam*</note>, and reflect upon them before you begin anything that might be harmful to you in the Work.

Now we shall set our hands to the Work. Take the powder of **Gold** and **Silver**, weigh it, and take just as much <term>Mercury of the Philosophers</term> <note>original: *Merc: Philosophorum*; a specialized, purified form of mercury central to alchemical theory, distinct from common quicksilver.</note>, as described above <note>original: *ut supra*</note>, and put it into

# On the Philosophical Stone

...to be read over often with a good understanding. Now that the redness has come forth, there is power in the Red Elixir to transmute all **imperfect metals** <term>imperfecta metalla</term> <gloss>base metals such as lead, tin, or copper</gloss> into true **Gold** <term>Aur.</term>—better even than that found in nature—withstanding all trials and **examinations** <term>examinationes</term> <note>standard metallurgical tests like cupellation or the touchstone</note>.

Furthermore, you should know that everything I shall report and teach here concerning this work is to be understood as the "Great Work," of which the **Philosophers** <term>Philosophi</term> have written with veiled words. Firstly, there is the **reduction of the body** <term>reductio corporis</term> into its first essence; that is, they amalgamated the **perfect body** <term>perfecte corpus</term>, which is **Silver** <term>☾</term> <note>the symbol for the Moon, representing silver</note>, because it is fixed; and this is the **solution** <term>solutio</term> of the Ancients. Their solution does not happen through the pouring on of rainwater, but with a dry **Water** <term>▽</term> <note>the symbol for water; "dry water" is a common alchemical term for Mercury</note> which is **Mercury** <term>Merc:</term>. This is the foundation of the Art, of which **Rasis** <note>Rhazes (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and alchemist</note> says: "Unless you **dissolve the body** <term>solvirt</term>, you work in vain." Of this solution, **Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), the foundational figure of Islamic and European alchemy</note> says in the **Book of Truth in the Turba** <term>libro Veritatis in turba</term>: "The surest way in the Art is **reduction** <term>reductio</term>," which is to say, that one **dissolves the bodies** <term>corpora solvirt</term> into a clear, beautiful water from which they were first generated; in this water are preserved **Spirit, Soul, and Body** <term>Spir. Anima & Corpus</term>.

**Geber** further says that when they had converted the **bodies** <term>corpora</term> into **Mercury** <term>Merc:</term> so that they might pass through a cloth <note>a method of straining liquid mercury to remove impurities</note>, they said: "Now we have an **Element** <term>Elem.</term> of Fire."

He says further that from the coarseness of the water, the Earth would be made; and they said: "We have also the other **Elements** <term>Elem.</term> such as **Water** <term>▽</term> and **Salt** <term>⊖</term> <note>the symbol for Salt or occasionally Earth</note>."

# Isaac Hollandus

The third step is **Purification** <term>Säuberung</term>, of which
**Morienus** <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary 7th-century hermit and alchemist said to have taught the secrets of the Stone to the King of Egypt</note> says: the earth is putrefied
with the water, and is made clean, and
then, through **God's** help, the

**And the Assembly of Philosophers** <note>original: "Et turba Philosophi," referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts</note> says: moisten the dry
with the wet, for the dry is the
Earth, and the wet is the **Water** <term>∇</term>; see, now
we have cleansed the Water and the **Earth** <term>⊖</term>.
The fourth step is the **evaporation of water** <note>original: "evaporatio aquae"</note>, as the
**Water of Paradise** <term>∇ paradisi</term> rises into the heavens and

this they call **the Scale** <term>♎</term> <note>The symbol for Libra, used here to represent the balance of the distillation process</note>, and through this rising and

before was only made into a paste in the
Earth; and this rising they

this is what the Philosophers say: when it is

and let it sublimate, until it becomes a

a calcined earth, sand, and ashes; and **Hermes**

for you will find at the bottom a shining

powder; then one must pour over it the **Paradise-Water**,
in which fine **Gold** <term>Aur.</term> shall be amalgamated,
and this pouring over they call **fermenting** <term>fermentiren</term>.

# On the Philosophical Stone

The Philosophers say further: moisten the **body** <note>original: "corpus"</note> with the **soul** <note>In alchemical theory, the "soul" usually refers to the permanent, coloring oil or essence extracted from a substance during the earlier stages of the work</note>; therefore, we add the soul to the **Stone**. For just as the human corpse is not without a soul, so too is our Stone not without soul and life. The soul cleanses the imperfect body, for it brings the **ferment** <term>Ferment</term> <note>A substance that, like yeast in dough, has the power to transform the nature of the entire mass into which it is introduced</note> to its nature. Therefore, they provide a ferment when the **Gold** <note>original: "Aur." for *Aurum*</note> is mixed with the **Water of Paradise** <note>Mentioned on the previous page as the distilled liquid that rises and falls like rain</note>. This is what **Morienus** says: unless you completely cleanse the powders and make them white, and afterwards pour the soul into them and unite them with one another, you have done or made nothing toward our Mastery.

Therefore, the Philosopher **Bautin** <note>Likely a reference to an authority cited in the *Turba Philosophorum* or similar compendiums</note> says: assemble the soul with the cleansed **body** and **spirit** <note>original: "corpore und spiritu"</note>; then its spirit is added to it, and they rejoice with it, for they are transformed from a single nature and from coarse things, and have now become subtle. And this is what the Philosophers say in the **Codex of Truth** <note>original: "codice veritatis"</note>: the **spirits** <note>original: "Spiritus"</note> are not gathered with the body before they have become completely free from all their impurity.

In this **union** <note>original: "conjunction"</note>, wonderful things happen, for all the colors of the whole world, as many as can be imagined, are enclosed within it; and finally, they all become one color, which is **red**. And then the body is colored with the ferment, which is the soul; and the spirit, along with the soul, is bound to the body and is transformed with it into the colors of the ferment.

...ments, so that then the fire may not separate it with any power nor strength.

From these related words, it is to be understood that the philosophers have spoken truly: our **Stone** is made of **Body**, **Soul**, and **Spirit**. And they have regarded the perfect **body** <note>original: "Corpus"</note> as a dead body, because the **Silver** <term>☽</term> <note>The symbol for Luna or Silver; in this context, it refers to a "sick" or imperfect metallic state that needs "fixing"</note> is sick and not fixed. And they say that the spirit is a running, shining water, and they have called the **soul** the **ferment** <term>Ferment</term> <note>An agent of transformation, like yeast in bread, that gives "life" and stability to the metallic body</note>. They speak the truth, for it gives life to the body that it did not have before, and brings it into a better form; and so everything is true which they have written with veiled words.

Some philosophers also say: unless you make the **corporeal** <note>Physical or solid</note> **incorporeal**, and the **incorporeal corporeal**, you have accomplished nothing in our art. We first make **Water** <term>▽</term> <note>The symbol for the element of Water</note> out of the body when it is **amalgamated** <note>Mixed or blended, usually with mercury</note>; and then the body is disembodied, and with constant **decoction** <term>Decoction</term> <note>A process of continuous heating to extract essence or achieve a state of concentrated purity</note> it becomes drunken and dies with the spirit; then the spirit becomes a body.

The ancients say: follow nature, and what you seek, you will find. This is true, for in our work we first make the dry from the wet, the subtle from the coarse, the coarse from the subtle, a spirit out of the body, and a body out of the spirit. From the highest we make the lowest, and from the lowest the highest; thus they transform one nature into another, just as has been heard.

# On the Philosopher’s Stone.

C v

But the reason that the Ancients wrote about such things with such dark words was for the sake of the unwise, who are not **sons of the art** <term>filii artis</term> <note>A traditional term for initiated alchemists or those devoted to the study of the "Great Work"</note>, so that they should not understand it, for it is meant to remain hidden.

Therefore, they also set down many chapters, so that the ignorant would think our art is very difficult; and in every chapter they mentioned many strange things and gave them many strange names, such as **reduction** <term>reductio</term>, **solution** <term>solutio</term>, **washing** <term>ablutio</term>, **sublimation** <term>sublimatio</term>, **distillation** <term>destilatio</term>, **coagulation** <term>coagulatio</term>, **calcination** <term>calcinatio</term>, **fixation** <term>fixatio</term>, and other **various names** <term>diversa nomina</term> and operations, as I have said before.

### Now observe how the Ancients
### found such names in the Work.

And if you wish to perform a work, just as the Ancients our ancestors did themselves, then take **Mercury** <term>☿</term> <note>The symbol for Quicksilver or Mercury; here, the author distinguishes "natural" mercury found near precious metal ores from laboratory-made versions</note>, which comes from the interior where Gold <term>☉</term> or Silver <term>☾</term> is found, and not the quicksilver that is made by artifice. Squeeze it through a leather cloth, and if anything remains in the leather, then see what kind of **body** <term>Corpus</term> <note>In alchemy, the "body" refers to the solid, physical matter of a metal</note> it is. Melt it in a crucible with borax, and you will find what metal is within it. If it is an **imperfect** metal <note>Metals like lead or tin, which alchemists believed were "diseased" or incomplete versions of gold</note>, then the mercury is of no use for our work. But if you find Gold <term>☉</term> and Silver <term>☾</term>, then it is good; and if you find no **body** at all, then it is also good.

Of such Mercury take as much as you wish; put it into a glass vessel, and an **alembic** <note>original: "albic"; likely referring to the cap or head of a distilling apparatus</note>

### Page 42

...upon it, and draw the remaining moisture from it, for this would be harmful to you in the work; afterwards take the **perfect body** <term>perfectè corpus</term> <note>A "perfect body" refers to a metal that has been fully purified, usually gold or silver</note> from the **cupel** <term>Capellen</term> <note>A shallow, porous bowl used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead</note> well-purged of **Lead** <term>♄</term> <note>The symbol for Saturn, representing Lead</note>, and amalgamate it with a large quantity of **Mercury** <term>☿</term>. The Ancients always took 3 parts **Silver** <term>☽</term> and 1 part **Gold** <term>☉</term>; this they called the **ferment** <term>ferment</term> <note>In alchemy, a ferment is a substance added to a base material to "leaven" it and transform it into a more precious state, much like yeast in bread</note>. Some of the Ancients amalgamated the Gold <term>☉</term> and Silver <term>☽</term> with one another, as taught above.

Others, however, amalgamated the Gold <term>☉</term> alone with the **Paradise-Water** <term>Paradieß-Wasser</term> <note>A poetic and technical name for a highly refined mercurial solvent or essence</note>, and finally poured it onto the prepared powder as a ferment, as shall follow hereafter. And when they had amalgamated the Silver <term>☽</term>, 

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of an alchemical vessel, possibly a cucurbit or flask, with a rounded base marked by horizontal lines and a narrow neck. It is placed alongside the middle lines of the text block.</image-desc>

they placed it in such a glass upon a **tripod** <term>tripodem</term> <note>A three-legged stand used to hold a flask over a heat source</note> in every way as taught in the previous work, with the same **regulation of the fire** <term>Regiment des Fewers</term> <note>The careful control and graduation of heat over time</note>. They likewise amalgamated the Gold <term>☉</term> and placed it in a glass upon a tripod, just like the Silver <term>☽</term>, maintaining the same regulation of the fire. And the Paradise-Water was from the Mercury <term>☿</term> from which its moisture had been drawn off; and they also put it into a glass and placed it with the other two glasses on the tripod, leaving them therein until they had to **wax** <term>inceriren</term> <note>From the Latin 'incerare'; the process of softening a dry alchemical substance to a wax-like consistency by adding liquid</note> the work. They did this so that it might be waxed the better and adhere to the work, for it becomes half-**fixed** <term>fix</term> <note>A "fixed" substance is one that can endure the heat of the fire without evaporating or changing</note> because it is **decocted** <term>Decoquirt</term> <note>Heated or boiled down over a long period to mature the substance</note> as long as the work itself; but it has not yet become powder, and even if it stood in the furnace for ten years, it would still not die.

Therefore **Mercury** <term>Mercurius</term> says, if a fish's ey— <note>original: "Fisch-Au-", likely "Fisch-Auge" (fish's eye), a traditional alchemical metaphor for a specific stage of visual clarity in the matter</note>

<summary>This page details the preparation of the alchemical "ferment" using amalgams of gold and silver refined from lead, and describes the use of "Paradise-Water" in a tripod furnace setup. It emphasizes the importance of long-term heating (decoction) to ensure the substance reaches a semi-fixed state without losing its vitality.</summary>

->### Concerning the Philosopher's Stone.<-

...is great / and is living through me, so I am not yet quite dead / and for this reason: because no **body** <term>corpus</term> <note>In alchemy, the "body" refers to the solid, physical matter of a metal, while the "spirit" refers to its volatile or vaporous state</note> has been mixed into it, neither Father nor Mother / nor anyone of its lineage / was added during **amalgamation** <term>amalgamiren</term> <note>The process of alloying a metal with mercury</note>. This you should understand thus / if a metal or a metallic spirit is mixed into it / or something from our **white and red Stone** <note>The "White Stone" and "Red Stone" represent the final stages of the alchemical work, capable of transmuting metals into silver and gold, respectively</note> / which are all of its lineage / then it dies immediately / but alone it never dies.

Dear children, read over this **lesson** <term>Lection</term> many times / so that you may learn to recognize the nature / of all things / and let this be enough said to you.

Now we will return again to our work / where we left it / they all three stand **on a tripod** <term>in tripode</term> <note>A three-legged stand used to support a vessel over a heat source</note>, in the innermost chamber / well **sealed** <term>lutiret</term> <note>To "lute" a vessel is to seal it hermetically with a clay-like paste to prevent the escape of vapors</note> / so that no air may escape / and they are governed with a small fire / such that one may hold a hand therein / without burning: for half an hour. Then the Ancients took counsel / how they wished to give a special sign to every sign / that they saw in the work / so that the fools should not understand it / who were not **sons of the art** <term>filii artis</term> <note>A traditional term for initiated alchemists who have mastered the secret language of the craft</note> / therefore they gave it so many different names / and established so many different manual operations / so that it should seem far too difficult / as shall follow hereafter / such as **dissolving** <term>solutio</term>, **solidifying** <term>coagulatio</term>, **wax-softening** <term>inceratio</term> <note>The process of bringing a substance to a waxy, soft consistency</note> and many other kinds of labor / and all solely for the sake of the ignorant / who are not children of the art / but rather bench-

->### Isaac Hollandus<-

...hard-hearted ones / who shall not possess the inheritance of the Wise.

First, the Ancients saw / that the **Matter** <term>Materia</term> or Stone gained a black skin or blackness on top / then they deliberated what they should call it / and found / that they should call it a **Blackening of the Sea** <term>Schwartz-Machung</term> or of the Lake / for the water was completely black on top / and because the **Sun** <term>☉</term> <note>Gold</note> and **Moon** <term>☽</term> <note>Silver</note> were within it / they called it the **Eclipse of the Sun and Moon** <note>original: "Ecclipſin ſolis & lunæ"; this refers to the *nigredo* or black stage of alchemy where the metals seem to lose their light</note> / and the fools did not understand it.

Secondly, they saw that the water which is **Mercury** <term>☿</term> / flew upward in the glass / out of the matter and the **bodies of the sun and moon** <note>original: "corporibus ſolis & lunæ"</note> / then they said / "the wind has carried him in his belly" <note>A famous reference to the *Emerald Tablet*, an ancient foundational text of alchemy</note> / because he rises from the Matter due to the heat.

Therefore a **Philosopher** <note>original: "Philoſophus"</note> says / you shall join him to the air / in whose belly he shall be carried / through the action of the Sun.

# On the Philosopher's Stone <note>original: "De lapide Philosophico"</note>

...which is its father; thereafter one shall give it to drink ☉ <note>Sun or Gold</note>. As it falls down again, upon the floor or **Matter** <term>Materia</term> / then one should drench it with the moisture of the Earth / which is its nourisher.

Of this, Hermes / a father of the Philosophers, says thus: **that which is above is like that which is below / and that which is below is like that which is above** <note>This is the famous opening of the *Emerald Tablet* (Tabula Smaragdina), the foundational text of Western alchemy</note>;

Dandin <note>Likely an alchemical authority, possibly a variation of Dandalus or a similar pseudonym</note> says / he who wishes to perform miracles with one thing / of which our Stone is the father / and the Earth is the sister. And the **Moon** <term>☽</term> <note>Silver</note> is the mother / and the wind carried it in its belly / and its nourisher is the Earth / furthermore the same Philosopher says / it ascends from the Earth into Heaven / and from Heaven descends again to the Earth / and shall take a strength / from that which is above / and from that which is below / and he speaks the truth / for when it begins to hang above in drops / and the same fall down again into the Earth / then it dies and becomes a powder / if it is now of this kind / then one should pour **Paradise-Water** <term>Paradieß-Wasser</term> <note>A term for a highly purified or "celestial" solvent, often distilled many times</note> over it / and then present the **Moon** <term>☽</term> <note>Silver</note> / which is then its nature / and thus pause for a long time / until your thing is perfected / then it is more precious than anything in the world / but the ignorant cannot understand it / and as they looked even closer / seeing that it ascended and descended even more in the glass / then they went to counsel and found no name / with which to call it / other than **sublimation** <term>sublimiren</term> <note>The process where a substance is heated into vapor and then condenses back into a solid or liquid, used here as a metaphor for spiritual and physical purification</note> / for it is a true sublimation.

Of this **Morienus** says in his books / our Stone is found in a foul, **corrupted monster** / there our Stone is gathered / because of this, some have understood / that one must seek the Stone in stables / mangers / filth / and manure / and they were deceived / for they are not our children <note>An alchemical idiom meaning these people are not true initiates or followers of the "Hermetic" art</note>.

### Isaac Hollandus

...give it back to drink. **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founding figure of alchemy</note> says: the earth shall be soaked with the water; **Morienus** and **Geber** <note>Geber refers to Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century alchemist</note> say: give the thirsty matter to drink until the time comes that it has enough of its juice, and in eternity it shall thirst no more. The ignorant do not understand that all this happens in a sealed glass, through long **decoction** <term>decoction: the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence</term> and tempered heat.

Then they saw that all the water was dry, and it turned into earth, which shall work according to our Work. **Geber** says: as our earth is, so is our mastery, for the greatest part is now completed. And they saw that the **matter** <note>original Latin: *Materia*</note> formed into a thick and hard substance, and that it stood firm and no longer rose up; then they said: this is a **perfect congealing** <note>original Latin: *perfecta cōgelatio*</note>, and it was of its own matter.

**Hermes** says: **dissolve** <note>original: *solvirt*</note> our stone, and **congeal** <note>original: *coagulirt*</note> it with great care; make it into earth, and thereafter white, thereafter living, and finally red—then you have the mastery. **Dandin** says: know that our art is nothing else than to perfectly dissolve and again congeal, without changing its substance, and without taking anything away or adding anything to it, until you have finished everything. Shun all other books and seek no more.

**Morienus** says: our Work is nothing else than a **work of women and a game of children** <note>A common alchemical phrase meaning that once the correct "fire" is set, the process is as simple as domestic chores or play</note>, and the uncomprehending cannot grasp it; they do not know how they should understand the hidden words, even if they [hear it] from our [children...]

It emphasizes the core alchemical principle of "Solve et Coagula" (dissolve and congeal) and lists the progression of colors toward the final red stage.</summary>

### Of the Philosopher's Stone

...if they were children, they would clearly understand and know everything.

Further they saw the earth, through long **decoction** <note>the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence</note> and tempered heat, become whiter and whiter, and at last they saw that it became perfectly glistening, and its whiteness surpassed all whiteness of the world. This whitening they called **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to achieve a change in its physical or chemical properties, often resulting in a fine powder</term>, and it is indeed the true **calcination** <note>original Latin: *calcinatio*</note> of the Ancients and the right name.

Therefore **Hermes** and **Morienus** say:
> Do not despise the ashes that lie at the bottom, for therein is a precious **diamond** <term>adamant: an ancient term for a diamond or an impenetrably hard substance</term>, and a maiden who shall be surrounded with fruits, and shall bring forth a son of life.

Now when the **leaven** <term>ferment: a substance added to the alchemical matter to transform its nature, much like yeast in bread</term> is to be mixed with the white earth, the imperfect **body** <term>Leichnamb: literally "corpse," used in alchemy to refer to the solid, lifeless matter before it is "animated" by the spirit</term> or earth receives the leaven; then that which is desired is accomplished. And this is what the Ancients mean when the **philosophers** <note>original: *Philoſophi*</note> say:
> Feed the child with its own milk, until it is grown.

This is to be understood as meaning that one should **wax** <term>inceriren: the process of softening a substance to a wax-like consistency by adding liquid, derived from the Latin *incerare*</term> the white earth with the leaven and **Paradise-Water**. Since this **matter** <note>original: *Materia*</note> was so white and true, they took it out of the furnace along with the other glass in which the **gold amalgam** <term>amalgamma solis: a mixture of gold and mercury</term> had been; they rubbed both together in a stone mortar with a steel pestle so that they blended well, and thus they fermented it.

Thereupon **Morienus** says: join and give to it

...its soul to it / so that it does not part from the **body** <term>Leichnamb: literally "corpse," referring here to the solid chemical residue or "dead" matter waiting to be reanimated by the spirit</term> in eternity; **Geber** <note>Geber: the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a legendary 8th-century Persian alchemist whose name was often used by later European writers to lend authority to their own texts</note> says / make a wedding / and she lays the bride in bed / with her groom / and sprinkles them both with **Heaven’s Dew** <note>Heaven's Dew: a poetic term for a refined alchemical liquid or distillate believed to have life-giving or "vivifying" properties</note> / and the bride shall conceive a son / who shall be a king over all his race / and all his enemies shall have peace with him / and he shall be crowned with a red diadem.

Afterward they sealed the glass / and placed it back in the furnace / and increased the fire a little / until the Paradise Water ascended and fell down again in drops upon the earth / this was the son who makes things shine / who shall be fed with his milk / until he is grown.

My child shall know and well understand / if you wish to make the **Red Stone** <note>The Red Stone is the final, most potent form of the Philosopher's Stone, capable of transmuting base metals into gold and acting as a universal medicine</note> / so you may bring the white earth to redness / with its **ferment** <term>ferment: a substance added to the alchemical matter to transform its nature, much like yeast in bread</term> and with the strengthening of the fire / until it becomes yellow / and when it is perfectly yellow / like **saffron** <note>original Latin: *crocus*</note>, then the **element of air** <note>original Latin: *Elementum aeris*</note> is turned outward / then the fire shall be noticeably increased / so the **element of fire** <note>original Latin: *Elementum Ignis*</note> shall come forth /

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the final stages of the alchemical Great Work.
